\section{Inequalities for tetrahedral eigenvalues}
\label{sec:ineqs_from_schur_weyl}

The purpose of this section is to develop the technical machinery used to prove numerous new inequalities constraining the set of tetrahedral eigenvalues.
Our approach relies heavily on the theory of irreducible representations of the group $\U(n)$ of unitary $n\times n$ matrices, or equivalently, the theory of polynomial irreducible representations of the group $\GL(n, \mathbb C)$ of invertible $n\times n$ matrices with complex entries.
More specifically, our approach relies on the decomposition of tensor powers $X^{\ot k}$ of $n\times n$ matrices provided by Schur-Weyl duality. 
In \cref{sec:schur_weyl} we review this prerequisite representation theory and provide elementary results regarding the decomposition of tensor powers $X^{\ot k}$ and then afterwards, in \cref{sec:eig_est_and_sep}, we describe how these decompositions of $X^{\ot k}$ encode information about the eigenvalues $\ex$ of $X$.
Building upon this machinery, in \cref{sec:binomial_theorem}, we state and prove a binomial theorem concerning various decompositions of tensors powers of matrix sums $(X+Y)^{\ot k}$.
Using the binomial theorem, in \cref{sec:new_ineq_horn_orig}, we will derive new inequalities constraining the set of eigenvalue triples $(\ya,\yb,\yc)$ of positive semidefinite matrices $(A,B,C)$ satisfying $A+B=C$ relevant for Horn's original eigenvalue problem.
Afterwards, in \cref{sec:new_ineq_horn_tet}, we again leverage the binomial theorem (or rather its multinomial generalization) to derive new inequalities constraining the set of eigenvalue sextuples $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ of positive semidefinite matrices $(A,B,C,D,E,F)$ satisfying $A+B=C$, $B+D=F$, $C+D=E$, and $A+F=E$ relevant for the tetrahedral Horn problem.

\subsection{Schur-Weyl duality}
\label{sec:schur_weyl}

The purpose of this section is to review the representation theory of $\U(n)$, or equivalently, the polynomial representation theory of $\GL(n)$.
The isomorphism classes of irreducible polynomial representations of $\GL(n)$ are indexed by Young diagrams.

A Young diagram $\yx$ of size $k$ is a collection of $k$ boxes arranged in rows of sorted length and can be identified with its sequence of non-zero row-lengths, $\yx \simeq (\yx_1, \ldots, \yx_\ell)$, where $\yx_i$ counts the number of boxes in the $i$th row, $\yx_1 \geq \cdots \geq \yx_\ell > 0$, and where $\sum_{i}\yx_i = k$.
The number of non-zero rows in $\yx$ is called the depth or length of $\yx$ and is denoted by $\ell(\yx)$ and the size of $\yx$ is denoted by $\syx$.
As a shorthand, we write $\yx \vdash_{n} k$ to denote a Young diagram $\yx$ of size $\syx = k$ with depth at most $\ell(\yx) \leq n$.
For example, $\yx = \ydiagram{4,2,1} \simeq (4,2,1)$ is a Young diagram of size $\syx = 4+2+1 = 7$ and depth $3$.

For each Young diagram $\ell$ of size $k$ with depth at most $n$, there is an irreducible polynomial representation of $\U(n)$ (or $\GL(n, \mathbb C)$), denoted by $(\pi_\yx, V_\yx^n)$, whose matrix coefficients are polynomials of homogeneous degree $k$ in the entries of $U \in \U(n)$.
The dimension of the $V_{\yx}^n$ is given by Weyl's dimension formula for $\U(n)$ \cite{hall2008representations,itzykson1966unitary},
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:weyl_dim}
    \dim(V_{\yx}^{n}) = \frac{\Delta(\yx + \rho)}{\Delta(\rho)} = \frac{\prod_{1 \leq i<j \leq n}(\yx_i - i - \yx_j + j)}{\prod_{1 \leq i<j \leq n}(j-i)},
\end{equation}
where $\rho \simeq (n-1, n-2, \ldots, 1, 0)$ is known as the Weyl vector and where 
\begin{equation}
    \Delta(x_1, \ldots, x_n) = \prod_{1 \leq i < j \leq n} (x_i-x_j).
\end{equation}
is known as the Vandermonde determinant.
The defining representation of $\U(n)$, wherein $U \in \U(n)$ acts via matrix-vector multiplication on $\mathbb C^{n}$, is associated to the Young diagram with one box, $\yx = \ydiagram{1} \simeq (1)$, and as dimension $\dim(V_{\ydiagram{1}}^{n}) = n$ as $V_{\ydiagram{1}}^{n} \simeq \mathbb C^{n}$.

The so-called Schur-Weyl decomposition of $(V_{\ydiagram{1}}^{n})^{\ot k} \simeq (\mathbb C^{n})^{\ot k}$ into irreducible representations of $\U(n)$ is as follows.
\begin{prop}[Schur-Weyl duality]
    Let $k$ and $n$ be positive integers.
    Then the decomposition of $(\mathbb C^{n})^{\ot k}$ into irreducible subrepresentations of $S_k \times \U(n)$, known as the \textit{Schur-Weyl} decomposition, is given by
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:schur_weyl_decomp}
        (\mathbb C^{n})^{\ot k} \cong \bigoplus_{\yx \vdash_{n} k} W_{\yx} \ot V_{\yx}^{n}
    \end{equation}
    where the direct summands are indexed by Young diagrams $\yx$ of size $k$ and depth at most $n$ rows, and where $W_{\yx}$ supports an irreducible representation of $S_k$ and $V_{\yx}^{n}$ supports the irreducible representation of $\U(n)$.
\end{prop}
The dimension of the irreducible representation $W_{\yx}$ of $S_k$ is given by
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:hook_dim}
    \dim(W_{\yx}) = \frac{\syx!}{H_\yx}, \qquad H_{\yx} = \prod_{b \in \yx} h_{\yx}(b),
\end{equation}
where the hook length, $h_{\yx}(b)$, of $b$ counts the number of boxes to the right and below $b$ plus one for itself.
We refer to $H_{\yx}$ as the hook-product for $\yx$.

Now let $(\pi_{\yx}, V_{\yx}^n)$ be an irreducible representation of $\U(n)$ labelled by the Young diagram $\yx$ with depth at most $n$.
Throughout this paper, the representation spaces $V_{\yx}^n$ are assumed to be equipped with respective Hermitian inner products making $\pi_{\yx}$ \textit{unitary} representations of $\U(n)$.
Similarly, if $(\sigma_{\yx}, W_{\yx})$ denotes the irreducible representation of $S_k$ labelled by $\yx$, we assume $W_{\yx}$ is equipped with a real inner product making $\sigma_{\yx}$ an \textit{orthogonal} representation of $S_k$.
In particular, the defining representation $V_{\ydiagram{1}} \simeq \mathbb C^{n}$ of $\U(n)$ is equipped with the standard Hermitian inner product on $\mathbb C^{n}$ making the standard basis $\{e_1, \ldots, e_n\}$ orthonormal.
Then for each $\yx$-isotypic subspace of $V_{\ydiagram{1}}^{\ot {\syx}} \simeq (\mathbb C^{n})^{\ot \syx}$, we let
\begin{equation}
    \iota_{\yx} : W_{\yx} \ot V_{\yx}^{n} \to V_{\ydiagram{1}}^{\ot {\syx}},
\end{equation}
denote the unique $S_k \times \U(n)$-equivariant map from $W_{\yx} \ot V_{\yx}^{n}$ to $V_{\ydiagram{1}}^{\ot {\syx}}$ which is additionally an isometry with respect to the choosen inner products on $W_{\yx}$, $V_{\yx}^{n}$ and $V_{\ydiagram{1}}$.

Then given an $n \times n$ matrix $X$, viewed as a linear operator on the defining representation $V_{\ydiagram{1}}^{n} \simeq \mathbb C^{n}$, let $\pi_{\yx}(X)$ be defined by
\begin{equation*}
    \pi_{\yx}(X) \coloneqq \frac{\Tr_{W_{\yx}}[\iota_{\yx}^{\dagger} X^{\ot {\syx}} \iota_{\yx}]}{\dim(W_{\yx})}.
\end{equation*}
Note that when $X$ is invertible, i.e. $X \in \GL(n,\mathbb C)$, the operator $\pi_{\yx}(X)$ as defined above coincides with the representation of $X$ on $V_{\yx}^{n}$, so this abuse of notation is permitted.
For any positive integer $k$, the $k$-th tensor power of $X$ then admits a decomposition as $X^{\ot k} \simeq \bigoplus_{\yx \vdash_n k} \pi_{\yx}(X)^{\oplus W_{\yx}}$, or more precisely,
\begin{equation}
    X^{\ot k} = \sum_{\yx \vdash_n k} \iota_{\yx} ( \pi_{\yx}(X) \ot \id_{W_{\yx}} ) \iota_{\yx}^{\dagger}.
\end{equation}
As a special case, when $\yx = \ydiagram{1}$, then $\pi_{\ydiagram{1}}(X) = X$.
Note also that the matrix coefficients of $\pi_{\yx}(X)$ are homogeneous degree $\abs{\yx}$ polynomials in the matrix coefficients of $X$ meaning for any scalar $s \in \mathbb C$,
\begin{equation}
    \pi_{\yx}(sX) = s^{{\syx}} \pi_{\yx}(X).
\end{equation}
Additionally, if $X,Y$ are two matrices, we have
\begin{equation}
    \pi_{\yx}(XY) = \pi_{\yx}(X)\pi_{\yx}(Y). 
\end{equation}
The corresponding character $\chi_{\yx}(X) = \Tr(\pi_{\yx}(X))$ of a representation of a Hermitian matrix $X$ with eigenvalues $\ex$ is equal to
\begin{equation*}
    \chi_{\yx}(X) \coloneqq \Tr(\pi_{\yx}(X)) = \frac{\Tr[P_{\yx} X^{\ot {\syx}}]}{\dim(W_{\yx})} = s_{\yx}(\ex),
\end{equation*}
where $P_{\yx} \coloneqq \iota_{\yx}^{\dagger} \iota_{\yx}$ is the orthogonal projection operator onto the $\yx$-isotypic subspace, and
where $s_{\yx}(\ex) = s_{\yx}(\ex_1, \ldots, \ex_n)$ is known as the Schur-polynomial associated to $\yx$.

% There are various formulas for the Schur-polynomial $s_{\yx}(\ex_1, \ldots, \ex_n)$.
% Here we present the Jacobi's bialternant formula
% \begin{equation}
%     s_{\yx}(\ex) = \frac{\det[\ex_i^{\yx_j+n-j}]}{\det[\ex_i^{n-j}]} = \frac{1}{\Delta(\ex)}\det[\ex_i^{(\yx+\rho)_j}].
% \end{equation}


\subsection{The binomial theorem}
\label{sec:binomial_theorem}

The purpose of this section is to state and prove the binomial theorem, which serves as the foundation for all subsequent results in this paper.
The binomial theorem expresses any polynomial irreducible representation of a matrix sum $X+Y$ as a sum over the polynomial irreducible representations of the summands $X$ and $Y$.

The theorem inherits its name from the non-commutative binomial expansion of the $k$-th tensor power of the sum $X+Y$,
\begin{equation}
    (X+Y)^{\ot k} = \sum_{\ell=0}^{k} \binom{k}{\ell} \left[\frac{1}{k!} \sum_{\sigma \in S_{k}} T(\sigma) (X^{\ot \ell} \ot Y^{\ot (k - \ell)})T(\sigma^{-1})\right]
\end{equation}
where $T(\sigma)$ denotes the operator which acts on $(\mathbb C^{n})^{\ot k}$ by a $\sigma$-permutation of the $k$ tensor factors of $\mathbb C^{n}$
For an example, consider the case where $k=3$:
\begin{align}
    \begin{split}
        (X+Y)^{\ot 3} 
        &= \underbrace{X \ot X \ot X}_{\ell=3} + \underbrace{X \ot X \ot Y + X \ot Y \ot X + Y \ot X \ot X}_{\ell=2} + \\
        &\qquad + \underbrace{X \ot Y \ot Y + Y \ot X \ot Y + Y \ot Y \ot X}_{\ell=1}  + \underbrace{Y \ot Y \ot Y}_{\ell=0}.
    \end{split}
\end{align}
As we have seen previously, the $k$-th tensor power of a matrix can also be decomposed into a sum over polynomial irreducible representations labelled by Young diagrams $\yc$ with sizes $\syc = k$.
Combining this formula with the non-commutative binomial expansion yields
\begin{equation}
    (X+Y)^{\ot k} = \sum_{\yc \vdash k} \iota_{\yc} (\pi_{\yc}(X+Y) \ot \id_{W_{\yc}}) \iota_{\yc}^{\dagger}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
    \dim (W_{\yc}) \pi_{\yc}(X+Y) = \sum_{\ell=0}^{k} \binom{k}{\ell} \Tr_{W_{\yc}}[\iota_{\yc}^{\dagger} (X^{\ot \ell} \ot Y^{\ot (k - \ell)}) \iota_{\yc}],
\end{equation}
where we have eliminated the symmetrization operation over $\sigma \in S_k$ because the partial trace over $W_{\yc}$ is $S_k$-invariant.
At this stage, for each summand indexed by $0 \leq \ell \leq k$, we can decompose $X^{\ot \ell}$ into representations of $X$ labelled by $\ya$ with sizes $\sya = \ell$ and $Y^{\ot \ell}$ into representations of $Y$ labelled by $\yb$ with sizes $\syb = k-\ell$.
\begin{align}
    X^{\ot \ell} = \sum_{\ya \vdash \ell} \iota_{\ya} (\pi_{\ya}(X) \ot \id_{W_{\ya}}) \iota_{\ya}^{\dagger}, \qquad
    Y^{\ot k-\ell} = \sum_{\yb \vdash k-\ell} \iota_{\yb} (\pi_{\yb}(Y) \ot \id_{W_{\yb}}) \iota_{\yb}^{\dagger}.
\end{align}
Upon doing so, one encounters the composite operator $\iota_{\yc}^{\dagger}(\iota_{\ya} \ot \iota_{\yb})$ which maps from $(V_{\ya}^{n} \ot W_{\ya}) \ot (V_{\yb}^{n} \ot W_{\yb})$ to $(V_{\yc}^{n} \ot W_{\yc})$ in an $S_\ell \times S_{k-\ell}$-equivariant and $\U(n)$-equivariant manner.
At this stage define multiplicity spaces $C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ and $\tilde C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ by
\begin{align}
    C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} &\coloneqq \Hom_{\U(n)}(V_{\yc}^{n}, V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n}), \\
    \tilde C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} &\coloneqq \Hom_{S_\ell \times S_{k-\ell}}(W_{\ya} \ot W_{\yb}, W_{\yc}),
\end{align}
where $C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ and $\tilde C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ are equipped with Hermitian inner products induced by the corresponding inner products on each of the representation spaces.
Furthermore, let $\iota^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} : C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \ot V_{\yc}^{n} \to V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n}$ be the $\U(n)$-equivariant isometry for the $\yc$-isotypic subspace of $V_{\ya}^{n}\ot V_{\yb}^{n}$ and $\omega^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} : C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \ot W_{\ya} \ot W_{\yb} \to W_{\yc}$ the $S_\ell \times S_{k-\ell}$-equivariant isometry for the $(\ya,\yb)$-isotypic subspace of $W_{\yc}$.

An important consequence of Schur-Weyl duality is an identification between $C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ and $\tilde C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$\footnote{At least whenever $\syc = k$, $\sya = \ell$ and $\syc = k - \ell$ and $\ell(\ya), \ell(\yb), \ell(\yc) \leq n$.} given by the map $T^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} : C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \to \tilde C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$, where
\begin{equation}
    T^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \coloneqq
    \frac{\Tr_{W_{\ya} \ot W_{\yb} \ot V_{\yc}^{n}}\left[
        (\omega^{\yc;\dagger}_{\ya\yb} \ot \id_{V_{\yc}^{n}}) 
        \circ (\iota_{\yc}^{\dagger})
        \circ (\iota_{\ya} \ot \iota_{\yb}) 
        \circ (\iota^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \ot \id_{W_{\ya} \ot W_{\yb}})
    \right]}{\dim(W_{\ya} \ot W_{\yb} \ot V_{\yc}^{n})}.
\end{equation}
Using Schur's lemma, one can show that $T^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ is actually an isometric and bijective map from $C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ and $\tilde C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$.
Combining everything together, we obtain
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:proto_binomial_theorem}
    \frac{\dim W_{\yc} \pi_{\yc}(X+Y)}{\syc!} = \sum_{\ya,\yb} \Tr_{C^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}} \left[ (\iota^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})^{\dagger} \frac{\dim W_{\ya} \pi_{\ya}(X)}{\sya!} \ot \frac{\dim W_{\ya} \pi_{\yb}(Y)}{\syb!} (\iota^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})\right],
\end{equation}
where we leave the summation over all pairs $(\ya,\yb)$ such that $\sya = \ell$, $\syb = k -\ell$ for $\syc = k$ implicit simply because the multiplicity space $C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ is empty if $\sya + \syb \neq \syc$ and thus the corresponding summand is taken to be zero.

In the remaining of the paper, we will use the above formula, \cref{eq:proto_binomial_theorem}, to prove numerous results concerning the eigenvalues of Hermitian matrices and their sums.
In order to simplify the presentation of our results considerably, we introduce the following definitions.
\begin{defn}[Hook-rescaled representations]
    Given an $n\times n$ matrix $X$ and a Young diagram $\yx$, define an operator $\Phi_{\yx}(X)$ acting on the representation space $V_{\yx}^{n}$ as:
    \begin{equation}
        \Phi_{\yx}(X) \coloneqq \frac{\pi_{\yx}(X)}{H_{\yx}},
    \end{equation}
    where $H_{\yx} = \prod_{b\in\yx} h_{\yx}(b)$ is the product of the Hook-lengths of the boxes in the Young diagram $\yx$.
    Furthermore, the trace of $\Phi_{\yx}(X)$ be
    \begin{equation}
        \vp_{\yx}(X) = \Tr[\Phi_{\yx}(X)] = \frac{s_{\yx}(x)}{H_{\yx}}.
    \end{equation}
\end{defn}

\begin{rem}
    The operator $\Phi_{\yx}(X)$ and trace $\vp_{\yx}(X)$ are simply rescalings of the representation matrix $\pi_{\yx}(X)$ of $X$ and the character $\chi_{\yx}(X)$ of $X$ respectively.
    This normalization scheme is such that summing the rescaled character over all Young diagrams $\yx$ with fixed size $k$ or over all sizes yields respectively
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\yx \vdash k} \vp_{\yx}(X) = \frac{\Tr(X)^k}{k!},
        \qquad
        \sum_{\yx} \vp_{\yx}(X) = \exp(\Tr(X)).
    \end{equation}
\end{rem}

Before stating the main result of this section, we first generalize the quantities $\Phi_{\yx}(X)$ and $\vp_{\yx}(X)$ to consider arguments involving more than one matrix.

\begin{defn}
    Given a pair $(X,Y)$ of $n\times n$ matrices and a triple of Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yc)$, define an operator $\Phi^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)$ acting on the representation space $V_{\yc}^{n}$ as
    \begin{equation}
        \Phi^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y) \coloneqq \Tr_{C^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}} [\iota^{\yc;\dagger}_{\ya,\yb} (\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi_{\yb}(Y)) \iota^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}],
    \end{equation}
    where $\iota^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} : C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \ot V_{\yc}^{n} \to V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n}$ is the $\U(n)$-equivariant isometry for the $\yc$-isotypic subspace of $V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n}$.
    Note the sum over Young diagrams $\ya$, and $\yb$ is implicitly restricted to those satisfying $\sya+\syb=\syc$, since otherwise the multiplicity space $C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ is zero-dimensional and thus the  summand is taken to be zero.
    Analogously, let the trace of $\Phi^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)$ be denoted by
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:varphi_double}
        \vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y) \coloneqq \Tr[\Phi^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)] = \Tr[P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}(\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi_{\yb}(Y))],
    \end{equation}
    where $P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} = \iota^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}\iota^{\yc;\dagger}_{\ya,\yb}$ is the projection operator onto the $\yc$-isotypic subspace of $V_{\ya}^{n}\ot V_{\yb}^{n}$.
\end{defn}

\begin{rem}
    While $\Phi_{\yx}(X)$ is a homogeneous polynomial in the matrix coefficients of $X$ of degree $\syx$ in the sense that $\Phi_{\yx}(sX) = s^{\syx} \Phi_{\yx}(X)$ holds for any $s \in \mathbb R$, $\Phi^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)$ is a bihomogeneous polynomial in $X$ and $Y$ of degrees $\sya$ and $\syb$ respectively in the sense that for any $s,t \in \mathbb R$,
    \begin{equation}
        \Phi^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(s X, t Y) = s^{\sya} t^{\syb} \Phi^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y).
    \end{equation}
\end{rem}

\begin{rem}
    \label{rem:trivial_yd}
    The quantity $\Phi^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)$ generalizes $\Phi_{\yx}(X)$ in the sense that one can take the triple $(\ya,\yb,\yc) = (\yx,\yempty,\yx)$ (where $\yempty$ denotes the Young diagram with zero boxes), in which case $V_{\yempty}^{n}$ supports the trivial one-dimensional representation with $\Phi_{\yempty}(Y) = 1$ and because $\dim(C^{\yx}_{\yx,\yempty}) = 1$,
    \begin{equation}
        \forall Y : \quad  \Phi^{\yx, \yempty}_{\yx}(X,Y) = \Phi_{\yx}(X).
    \end{equation}
\end{rem}

With these definitions in place, we are in a position to state the binomial theorem.
\begin{thm}[Binomial theorem]
    \label{thm:binomial}
    For all $n\times n$ matrices $X,Y$, and all Young diagrams $\yc$,
    \begin{equation}
        \Phi_{\yc}(X+Y) = \sum_{\ya,\yb} \Phi^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y),
    \end{equation}
    where the sum is implicitly taken over all Young diagrams such that $\sya + \syb = \syc$.
\end{thm}
\begin{proof}
    See preceding discussion.
\end{proof}

Taking the trace of the operators in \cref{thm:binomial} produces the following corollary which pertains to sums of the quantity $\vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)$ (\cref{eq:varphi_double}).
\begin{cor}
    \label{cor:bisummation}
    Let $X,Y$ be $n\times n$ matrices. Then the following identities hold:
    \begin{align}
        \forall \ya,\yb : \quad \sum_{\yc} \vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y) &= \vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yb}(Y), \\
        \forall \yc : \quad \sum_{\ya,\yb} \vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y) &= \vp_{\yc}(X+Y).
    \end{align}
\end{cor}
\begin{proof}
    The first identity involving a summation over $\ya$ and $\yb$ is simply the result of taking the trace of both sides of \cref{thm:binomial}.
    For the second identity, involving a summation over $\yc$, follows from the resolution of the identity $\sum_{\yc \vdash \sya+\syb} P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} = \id_{V_{\ya}^{n}\ot V_{\yb}^{n}}$:
    \begin{equation}
        \vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yb}(Y) = \Tr[\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi_{\yb}(Y)] = \sum_{\yc} \Tr[P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}(\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi_{\yb}(Y))] = \sum_{\yc} \vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y),
    \end{equation}
    where the last equality is just the definition of $\vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)$. 
    Once again, the condition that $\syc = \sya + \syb$ is implicitly present.
\end{proof}

\begin{rem}
    One might consider what happens to the operator $\Phi^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)$ when one sums over $\yc$ instead of $(\ya,\yb)$.
    However, an analogous identity concerning a sum over $\yc$ of the operator $\Phi^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)$ does not immediately follow because the tensor product $\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi_{\yb}(Y)$ is not necessarily block diagonalizable into $\yc$-isotypic subspaces in any obvious way.
    One exception occurs when $X = Y$, in which case,
    \begin{equation}
        \pi_{\ya}(X) \ot \pi_{\yb}(X)
        = \sum_{\yc} \iota_{\ya\yb}^{\yc} (\pi_{\yc}(X) \ot \id_{C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}}) \iota_{\ya\yb}^{\yc;\dagger},
    \end{equation}
    which is the operator version of the character product formula $\chi_{\ya}(X)\chi_{\yb}(X) = \sum_{\yc} c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \chi_{\yc}(X)$.
\end{rem}

\subsection{Inequalities for Horn's original problem}
\label{sec:new_ineq_horn_orig}

In \cref{sec:binomial_theorem} we introduced to the quantity $\vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)$ (\cref{eq:varphi_double}) and saw in \cref{cor:bisummation} that it satisfied two summation identities.
Under the assumption that the matrices $X$, and $Y$ are positive semidefinite, we can conclude that $\vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)$ is non-negative also because $\vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y) = \Tr[P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} (\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi_{\yb}(Y))]$ where $P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ is a projection operator and $\Phi_{\ya}(X),\Phi_{\yb}(Y) \geq 0$ because $X,Y \geq 0$.
Moreover, we note that $\vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y) = 0$ whenever the Littlewood-Richardson coefficient $c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ vanishes.
These observations imply the following necessary condition for compatibility of eigenvalues.
\begin{prop}
    \label{prop:coupling_condition}
    Let $(a,b,c)$ be a triple of non-negative eigenvalues.
    Then there exists positive semidefinite $n\times n$ matrices $(A,B,C)$ satisfying $A+B=C$ with eigenvalues $(a,b,c)$ only if for every triple of Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yc)$ there exists a non-negative $\Gamma^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \geq 0$ such that $\Gamma^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} = 0$ whenever $c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} = 0$ and
    \begin{align}
        \forall \ya,\yb : \quad \sum_{\yc} \Gamma^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} = \vp_{\ya}(\ea)\vp_{\yb}(\eb),
        \qquad \text{and} \qquad 
        \forall \yc : \quad \sum_{\ya,\yb} \Gamma^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} = \vp_{\yc}(\ec).
    \end{align}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    The claim follows directly from \cref{cor:bisummation} after identifying $\Gamma^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} = \vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(A,B) \geq 0$.
\end{proof}
\begin{cor}
    \label{cor:row_col_horn_ineqs}
    Let $(a,b,c)$ be the eigenvalues of positive semidefinite $n\times n$ matrices $(A,B,C)$ satisfying $A+B=C$. Then,
    \begin{align}
        \forall \ya,\yb : \quad \vp_{\ya}(\ea)\vp_{\yb}(\eb) \leq \sum_{\substack{\yc\\c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} > 0}}\vp_{\yc}(\ec), 
        \qquad \text{and} \qquad 
        \forall \yc : \quad \vp_{\yc}(\ec) \leq \sum_{\substack{\ya,\yb\\c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} > 0}}\vp_{\ya}(\ea)\vp_{\yb}(\eb).
    \end{align}
\end{cor}
\begin{proof}
    The claim follows from \cref{prop:coupling_condition} and noting that whenever $\Gamma^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} > 0$ (only when $c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} > 0$) we have the bound $\Gamma^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \leq \min\{\vp_{\ya}(\ea)\vp_{\yb}(\eb), \vp_{\yc}(\ec)\}$ for all triples $(\ya,\yb,\yc)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{rem}
    Using \cref{prop:coupling_condition}, it is possible to prove even stronger inequalities than those appearing in \cref{cor:row_col_horn_ineqs} by examining the set of triples of Young diagrams $(\ya, \yb, \yc)$ where $c^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb} \neq 0$.
    We illustrate this point with an example.
    Let $k = \sya + \syb = \syc = 4$, then the following is the table of values for $c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$.
    \begin{center}
        \newcommand{\zero}{\cellcolor{white}{\color{gray}$0$}}
        \newcommand{\numb}[1]{\cellcolor{black!80!white}{\color{white}$#1$}}
        \begin{tabular}{|cc||c|c|c|c|c||c|c|c||c|c|c|c||c|c|c||c|c|c|c|c|}
            \hline
            $c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ & $\ya$ & 
            $\ydiagram{4}$ & $\ydiagram{3,1}$ & $\ydiagram{2,2}$ & $\ydiagram{2,1,1}$ & $\ydiagram{1,1,1,1}$ &
            $\ydiagram{3}$ & $\ydiagram{2,1}$ & $\ydiagram{1,1,1}$ &
            $\ydiagram{2}$ & $\ydiagram{2}$ & $\ydiagram{1,1}$ & $\ydiagram{1,1}$ &
            $\ydiagram{1}$ & $\ydiagram{1}$ & $\ydiagram{1}$ &
            $\ydiagram{}$ & $\ydiagram{}$ & $\ydiagram{}$ & $\ydiagram{}$ & $\ydiagram{}$ \\
            $\yc$ & $\yb$ & 
            $\ydiagram{}$ & $\ydiagram{}$ & $\ydiagram{}$ & $\ydiagram{}$ & $\ydiagram{}$ &
            $\ydiagram{1}$ & $\ydiagram{1}$ & $\ydiagram{1}$ &
            $\ydiagram{2}$ & $\ydiagram{1,1}$ & $\ydiagram{2}$ & $\ydiagram{1,1}$ &
            $\ydiagram{3}$ & $\ydiagram{2,1}$ & $\ydiagram{1,1,1}$ &
            $\ydiagram{4}$ & $\ydiagram{3,1}$ & $\ydiagram{2,2}$ & $\ydiagram{2,1,1}$ & $\ydiagram{1,1,1,1}$ \\ \hline\hline
            $\ydiagram{4}$ & & 
            \numb{1} & \zero & \zero & \zero & \zero & 
            \numb{1} & \zero & \zero & 
            \numb{1} & \zero & \zero & \zero & 
            \numb{1} & \zero & \zero &
            \numb{1} & \zero & \zero & \zero & \zero 
            \\\hline
            $\ydiagram{3,1}$ &  & 
            \zero & \numb{1} & \zero & \zero & \zero & 
            \numb{1} & \numb{1} & \zero & 
            \numb{1} & \numb{1} & \numb{1} & \zero & 
            \numb{1} & \numb{1} & \zero & 
            \zero & \numb{1} & \zero & \zero & \zero 
            \\\hline
            $\ydiagram{2,2}$ &  & 
            \zero & \zero & \numb{1} & \zero & \zero & 
            \zero & \numb{1} & \zero & 
            \numb{1} & \zero & \zero & \numb{1} & 
            \zero & \numb{1} & \zero & 
            \zero & \zero & \numb{1} & \zero & \zero 
            \\\hline
            $\ydiagram{2,1,1}$ &  & 
            \zero & \zero & \zero & \numb{1} & \zero & 
            \zero & \numb{1} & \numb{1} & 
            \zero & \numb{1} & \numb{1} & \numb{1} &
            \zero & \numb{1} & \numb{1} &
            \zero & \zero & \zero & \numb{1} & \zero 
            \\\hline
            $\ydiagram{1,1,1,1}$ &  & 
            \zero & \zero & \zero & \zero & \numb{1} & 
            \zero & \zero & \numb{1} & 
            \zero & \zero & \zero & \numb{1} & 
            \zero & \zero & \numb{1} & 
            \zero & \zero & \zero & \zero & \numb{1} 
            \\\hline
        \end{tabular}
    \end{center}
    Using this table, one sees the inequalities in \cref{cor:row_col_horn_ineqs} are associated to single columns or single rows, e.g., along the $(\ya,\yb) = (\ydiagram{2}, \ydiagram{1,1})$, we obtain
    \begin{equation}
        \vp_{\ydiagram{2}}(X)\vp_{\ydiagram{1,1}}(Y) \leq \vp_{\ydiagram{3,1}}(X+Y) + \vp_{\ydiagram{2,1,1}}(X+Y).
    \end{equation}
    However, using \cref{prop:coupling_condition}, a stronger inequality is obtained by including all columns covered by the rows associated to the right-hand side of the above inequality, e.g.,
    \begin{equation}
        \vp_{\ydiagram{3,1}}(X) + \vp_{\ydiagram{2,1,1}}(X) + 
        \vp_{\ydiagram{2}}(X)\vp_{\ydiagram{1,1}}(Y) + \vp_{\ydiagram{1,1}}(X)\vp_{\ydiagram{2}}(Y) +
        \vp_{\ydiagram{3,1}}(Y) + \vp_{\ydiagram{2,1,1}}(Y)
        \leq \vp_{\ydiagram{3,1}}(X+Y) + \vp_{\ydiagram{2,1,1}}(X+Y).
    \end{equation}
    In general, \cref{prop:coupling_condition} describes a linear system of inequality constraints in the variables $\Gamma^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}, \vp_{\ya}(\ea)\vp_{\yb}(\eb),$ and $\vp_{\yc}(\ec)\}$ which, in principle, be solved for $\vp_{\ya}(\ea)\vp_{\yb}(\eb)$ and $\vp_{\yc}(\ec)$ by standard linear quantifier elimination techniques such as Fourier-Motzkin elimination \cite{dantzig1972fourier}.
    While these additional inequalities are certainly interesting to explore, for the purposes of the original Horn problem, we will later prove, in \cref{prop:approx_tri}, that it is sufficient to restrict our attention to inequalities of the form \cref{cor:row_col_horn_ineqs}.
\end{rem}



\subsection{The \texorpdfstring{$\boldsymbol{6j}$}{6j} symbol for \texorpdfstring{$\boldsymbol{\U(n)}$}{U(n)}}
\label{sec:sixj_tet}

The purpose of this section is to introduce a representation theoretic object, namely the $6j$ symbol, and then in the subsequent sections show how it places constraints on whether a given sextuple of eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ is tetrahedral in a manner which generalizes how the Littlewood-Richardson coefficient encodes whether a given triple $(\ea,\eb,\ec)$ is trigonal.
For the case where $n=2$, this correspondence is well-known \cite{roberts1999classical,ponzano1968semiclassical,backens2010sixjsymbols}, while for the case where $n \geq 3$, this correspondence was already established indirectly by \citeauthor{christandl2018recoupling} \cite{christandl2018recoupling}.
Here we establish this correspondence directly.

As we discussed in the introduction, the $6j$ symbol emerges naturally when considering successive decompositions of the triple tensor product $V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n} \ot V_{\yd}^{n}$.
\begin{defn}
    \label{defn:sixj}
    Let $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ be a sextuple of Young diagrams with sizes satisfying
    \begin{equation}
        \sya + \syb = \syc, \qquad \syb + \syd = \syf, \qquad \syc + \syd = \sye, \qquad \sya + \syf = \sye.
    \end{equation}
    The \defnsty{$\U(n)$-$6j$ operator} is the operator
    \begin{equation}
        \abcdefsymbol
        :
        C^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb} \ot C^{\ye}_{\yd,\yc} \to C^{\ye}_{\ya, \yf} \ot C^{\yf}_{\yb,\yd},
    \end{equation}
    defined by
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:sixj}
        \abcdefsymbol
        \coloneqq
        \frac{1}{\dim(V_{\ye})}
        \Tr_{V_{\ye}}[
        (\iota_{\ya\yf}^{\ye} \ot \id_{C^{\yf}_{\yb\yd}})^{\dagger}
        (\id_{V_{\ya}^n}\ot \iota_{\yb\yd}^{\yf})^{\dagger}
        (\iota_{\ya\yb}^{\yc}\ot\id_{V_{\yd}^n})
        (\id_{C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}} \ot \iota_{\yc\yd}^{\ye})
        ].
    \end{equation}
    %and depicted diagrammatically by
    %\begin{equation}
    %    \tikzfig{tikz/final/sixj_defn}.
    %\end{equation}
    %\footnotetext{It is important to note that there does not seem to exist a universal convention concerning how $6j$ symbols are normalized; \citeauthor{coquereaux2023integer} discusses is problem in \cite{coquereaux2023integer}.}.
\end{defn}
To illustrate the situation more concretely, consider the following three projection operators on the triple tensor product $V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n} \ot V_{\yd}^{n}$:
\begin{equation}
    P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}\ot \id_{V_{\yd}^{n}}, \qquad P^{\ee}_{\ya\yb\yd}, \qquad \id_{V_{\ya}^{n}} \ot P^{\yf}_{\yb\yd}.
\end{equation}
In general, $P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ and $P^{\yf}_{\yb\yd}$ do not necessarily commute with each other in the sense that for some values of $\ya,\yb,\yd,\yc,$ and $\yf$, one observes
\begin{equation}
    [P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}\ot \id_{V_{\yd}^{n}}, \id_{V_{\ya}^{n}} \ot P^{\yf}_{\yb\yd}] \neq 0.
\end{equation}
Nevertheless, the projection operators $P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ and $P^{\yf}_{\yb\yd}$ each respectively commute with $P^{\ye}_{\ya\yb\yd}$ in the sense that for all $\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,$ and $\yf$,
\begin{equation}
    [P^{\ye}_{\ya\yb\yd}, P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}\ot \id_{V_{\yd}^{n}}] = 0, \qquad
    [P^{\ye}_{\ya\yb\yd}, \id_{V_{\ya}^{n}} \ot P^{\yf}_{\yb\yd}] = 0.
\end{equation}
Let $\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc}$, and $\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf}$ be Hermitian projection operators $V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n} \ot V_{\yd}^{n}$ given by
\begin{align}
    \begin{split}
        \label{eq:double_projs}
        \Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc} 
        &\coloneqq P^{\ye}_{\ya\yb\yd} (P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \ot \id_{V_{\yd}^{n}}) 
        = (\iota_{\ya\yb}^{\yc}\ot\id_{V_{\yd}^n})(P^{\ye}_{\yc\yd} \ot \id_{C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}})(\iota_{\ya\yb}^{\yc}\ot\id_{V_{\yd}^n})^{\dagger}, \\
        \Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf} 
        &\coloneqq P^{\ye}_{\ya\yb\yd} (\id_{V_{\ya}^{n}} \ot P^{\yf}_{\yb\yd})
        = (\id_{V_{\ya}^n}\ot \iota_{\yb\yd}^{\yf})(P^{\ye}_{\ya\yf} \ot \id_{C^{\yf}_{\yb\yd}})(\id_{V_{\ya}^n}\ot \iota_{\yb\yd}^{\yf})^{\dagger}, \\
    \end{split}
\end{align}

For any operator $O : V_1 \to V_2$ between Hermitian vector spaces $V_1$ and $V_2$, we define operator norms $\norm{O}_{\infty} = \max_{v \in V_1, \norm{v}_{V_1} = 1} \norm{Ov}_{V_2}$ and $\norm{O}_{2} = \Tr_1[O^{\dagger} O]^{\frac{1}{2}} = \Tr_2[O O^{\dagger}]^{\frac{1}{2}}$.
\begin{prop}
    \label{prop:6j_norm_double_proj}
    For any sextuple of Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:6j_norm_equivalences}
        \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{\infty} = \norm{\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc}\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf}}_{\infty}, \qquad
        \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{2}^{2} = \frac{1}{\dim V_{\ye}^{n}}\Tr[\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc}\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf}].
    \end{equation}
    %where $\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc}$ and $\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf}$ defined in \cref{eq:double_projs}.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    By definition, the product $\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc}\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf}$ is an $\U(n)$-equivariant map on $V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n} \ot V_{\yd}^{n}$ supported on the $\ye$-isotypic subspace $V_{\ye}^{n} \ot C^{\ye}_{\ya\yb\yd}$.
    By Schur's lemma and \cref{defn:sixj}, we conclude
    \begin{equation}
        \Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc}\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf} =  
        (\id_{V_{\ya}^n}\ot \iota_{\yb\yd}^{\yf})
        (\iota_{\ya\yf}^{\ye} \ot \id_{C^{\yf}_{\yb\yd}})
        (\abcdefsymbol \ot V_{\ye}^{n})
        (\id_{C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}} \ot \iota_{\yc\yd}^{\ye})^{\dagger}
        (\iota_{\ya\yb}^{\yc}\ot\id_{V_{\yd}^n})^{\dagger}.
    \end{equation}
    The norm conditions in \cref{eq:6j_norm_equivalences} then follow because the norms $\norm{O}_{\infty}$ and $\norm{O}_{2}$ are invariant under embedding $O$ into larger spaces using isometries and moreover that $\norm{O\ot \id_V}_{\infty} = \norm{O}_{\infty}$ for any vector space $V$.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Inequalities for the tetrahedral Horn problem}
\label{sec:new_ineq_horn_tet}

In \cref{sec:new_ineq_horn_orig}, we saw how the quantity $\vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)$ (\cref{eq:varphi_double}) could be used to produce inequalities applicable to the original Horn problem.
In this section, we generalize this analysis to produce inequalities applicable to the tetrahedral Horn problem using a generalization of $\vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)$ which depends on three matrices.
\begin{defn}
    Let $X,Y,Z$ be $n\times n$ matrices.
    For any sextuple $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ of Young diagrams, let
    \begin{equation}
        \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z) \coloneqq \Tr[(P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}\ot \id_{V_{\yd}^{n}})P^{\ye}_{\ya\yb\yd}(\id_{V_{\ya}^{n}} \ot P^{\yf}_{\yb\yd})\Phi_{\ya}(X)\ot\Phi_{\yb}(Y)\ot\Phi_{\yd}(Z)].
    \end{equation}
\end{defn}
Remark that in our definition of $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$, we permit the sizes of the involved Young diagrams to be arbitrary.
However, there are numerous conditions on the sizes of the Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ which are necessary (but not always sufficient) for $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$ to be non-zero; specifically,
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:yd_sizes_tet}
    \sya + \syb = \syc, \qquad \syb + \syd = \syf, \qquad \sya + \syf = \sye, \qquad \syc + \syd = \sye.
\end{equation}
If we additionally let 
\begin{equation}
    \Omega_{\ya\yb\yd}^{X,Y,Z} \coloneqq \Phi_{\ya}(X)\ot\Phi_{\yb}(Y)\ot\Phi_{\yd}(Z).
\end{equation}
then we can compactly express $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$ as
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:varphi_compact_tet}
    \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z) = \Tr[\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc}\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf}\Omega_{\ya\yb\yd}^{X,Y,Z}].
\end{equation}

Unlike the quantity $\vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y) = \Tr[P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi_{\yb}(Y)]$ involving two matrices $X,Y$ (see \cref{sec:binomial_theorem}), the quantity defined here, $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$, is not necessarily non-negative or even real valued even under the assumption that $(X,Y,Z)$ are positive semidefinite.
Nevertheless, summing over either $\yc$ or $\yf$ restores non-negativity because it eliminates one of the potentially non-commuting projection operators $\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc}$ or $\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf}$.
We denote these partial sums of $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$ over $\yc$ and $\yf$ respectively as
\begin{align}
    \label{eq:psum_defns}
    \begin{split}
        \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_ \ye\yf}(X,Y,Z) &\coloneqq \sum_{\yc}\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z) = \Tr[\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf}\Omega_{\ya\yb\yd}^{X,Y,Z}] = \Tr[P^{\ye}_{\yc\yd} (\Phi^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y) \ot \Phi_{\yd}(Z))], \\
        \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye \_}(X,Y,Z) &\coloneqq \sum_{\yf}\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z) = \Tr[\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc}\Omega_{\ya\yb\yd}^{X,Y,Z}] = \Tr[P^{\ye}_{\ya\yf} (\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi^{\yb\yd}_{\yf}(Y,Z))],
    \end{split}
\end{align}
where the sum over $\yc$ is implicitly such that $\syc = \sya + \syb$ and the sum over $\yf$ is implicitly such that $\syf = \syb + \syd$.
Although $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$ is not necessarily real or positive, the above expression makes it evident that
\begin{equation}
    \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z) \geq 0, \qquad\qquad \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye \_}(X,Y,Z) \geq 0.
\end{equation}
The various summation identities satisfied by \cref{fig:sum_ident} are summarized in \cref{fig:sum_ident}.
\begin{figure}
    \scalebox{0.9}{\tikzfig{tikz/final/partial_sums}}
    \caption{Various summation identities satisfied by $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$.}
    \label{fig:sum_ident}
\end{figure}

The next series of results concerns upper bounds on the absolute value of $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$.
These upper bounds follow from the following general lemma applied to the compact expression for $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$ appearing in \cref{eq:varphi_compact_tet}.
\begin{prop}
    \label{prop:cauchy_schwarz_ineq_double_proj}
    Let $\Omega \geq 0$ be a positive semidefinite operator and let $\Pi_1, \Pi_2$ be Hermitian projection operators acting on a common vector space.
    Then
    \begin{equation}
        \abs{\Tr(\Pi_1\Pi_2\Omega)} \leq \norm{\Pi_1\Pi_2}_{\infty} \Tr(\Pi_1\Omega)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Tr(\Pi_2\Omega)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
    \end{equation}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    As $\Omega \geq 0$ is positive semidefinite, let $\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}} \geq 0$ denote its square root. 
    Then let $L = \Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_2\Pi_1$ and $R = \Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_1$ so that
    \begin{equation}
        \braket{L,R} = \Tr(L^{\dagger} R) = \Tr(\Pi_1\Pi_2\Omega\Pi_1) = \Tr(\Pi_1\Pi_2\Omega),
    \end{equation}
    where the last equality uses the assumption that $\Pi_1^2 = \Pi_1$.
    An application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product $\braket{L,R}$ then implies
    \begin{equation}
        \abs{\Tr(\Pi_1\Pi_2\Omega)}^2 = \abs{\braket{L,R}}^2 \leq \Tr[L^{\dagger}L]\Tr[R^{\dagger}R] = \Tr[\Pi_1\Pi_2\Omega\Pi_2\Pi_1]\Tr[\Pi_1\Omega\Pi_1] = \Tr[\Pi_1\Pi_2\Omega\Pi_2]\Tr[\Pi_1\Omega].
    \end{equation}
    Now let $\{ v_j \}_{j}$ be an orthonormal eigenbasis for $\Pi_2\Omega\Pi_2$ with eigenvalue $\omega_j \geq 0$.
    Then, because it was assumed that $\Pi_2^{2} = \Pi_2$, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \Tr[\Pi_1\Pi_2\Omega\Pi_2] = \Tr[(\Pi_2\Pi_1^2\Pi_2)(\Pi_2\Omega\Pi_2)] = \sum_{j} \omega_j \braket{\Pi_1\Pi_2 v_j,\Pi_1\Pi_2v_j} \leq \sum_{j} \omega_j \norm{\Pi_1\Pi_2}_{\infty}^{2} = \Tr[\Pi_2\Omega]\norm{\Pi_1\Pi_2}_{\infty}^{2}.
    \end{equation}
    The claim then follows from combining these inequalities and taking the square root of both sides.
\end{proof}
\begin{lem}
    \label{lem:varphi_tet_main_ineq}
    Let $(X,Y,Z)$ be positive semidefinite $n\times n$ matrices.
    Then for all sextuples of Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$,
    \begin{equation}
        \abs{\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)} \leq \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{\infty} \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)^{\frac{1}{2}} \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\_}(X,Y,Z)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
    \end{equation}
\end{lem}
\begin{proof}
    Under the assumption that $X,Y,Z \geq 0$, we have that $\Omega_{\ya\yb\yd}^{X,Y,Z} = \Phi_{\ya}(X)\ot\Phi_{\yb}(Y)\ot\Phi_{\yd}(Z) \geq 0$ is positive semidefinite also.
    Now applying \cref{prop:cauchy_schwarz_ineq_double_proj} to the compact expression for $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$ in \cref{eq:varphi_compact_tet} yields
    \begin{equation}
        \abs{\Tr[\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc}\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf}\Omega_{\ya\yb\yd}^{X,Y,Z}]}
        \leq \norm{\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc}\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf}}_{\infty} \Tr[\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yc}\Omega_{\ya\yb\yd}^{X,Y,Z}]^{\frac{1}{2}}\Tr[\Pi_{\ya\yb\yd}^{\ye;\yf}\Omega_{\ya\yb\yd}^{X,Y,Z}]^{\frac{1}{2}}
    \end{equation}
    which is equivalent to the claim due to \cref{prop:6j_norm_double_proj}.
\end{proof}
Our next proposition enables us to place bounds on $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$ and $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\_}(X,Y,Z)$ which depend only the eigenvalues of $X,Y,X+Y,Z,X+Y+Z$, and $Y+Z$ and not their eigenvectors.
\begin{prop}
    \label{prop:tet_psum_ineq}
    Let $X,Y,Z \geq 0$ be positive semidefinite $n\times n$ matrices.
    Then we have bounds on the partial sums,
    \begin{align}
        \label{eq:psum_prod_bounds}
        \begin{split}
            \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z) &\leq \vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yf}(Y+Z),\\
            \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\_}(X,Y,Z) &\leq \vp_{\yc}(X+Y)\vp_{\yd}(Z),
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
    as well as bounds on $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\ye}(X,Y,Z)$:
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:psum_yc_ye_uniform}
        \max\{\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\_}(X,Y,Z), \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)\} \leq \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\ye}(X,Y,Z) \leq \min\{\vp_{\ye}(X+Y+Z),\vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yb}(Y)\vp_{\yd}(Z)\}.
    \end{equation}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    Each of these bounds are consequences of the summation identities for $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$ and the fact that the partial sums $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$ and $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\_}(X,Y,Z)$ are non-negative real-valued whenever $X,Y,Z \geq 0$ are positive semidefinite.
    These summation identities are summarized in \cref{fig:sum_ident}.
    To begin, the quantity $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$ (defined in \cref{eq:psum_defns}) can be summed over $\ye$ to obtain:
    \begin{align}
        \label{eq:psum_yf_over_ye}
        \begin{split}
            \sum_{\ye} \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z) 
            &= \sum_{\ye}\Tr[P^{\ye}_{\ya\yf} (\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi^{\yb\yd}_{\yf}(Y,Z))] 
            = \Tr[\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi^{\yb\yd}_{\yf}(Y,Z)] 
            = \vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yf}^{\yb\yd}(Y,Z).
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
    Further summing over $\yb$ and $\yd$ and applying \cref{cor:bisummation} produces
    \begin{align}
        \begin{split}
            \sum_{\ye\yb\yd} \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z) 
            &= \vp_{\ya}(X)\sum_{\yb\yd}\vp_{\yf}^{\yb\yd}(Y,Z).
            = \vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yf}(Y+Z).
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
    The other summation identities are analogous.
    Now using the non-negativity of $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$, we obtain the first claimed inequality in \cref{eq:psum_prod_bounds}.
    The second claimed inequality in \cref{eq:psum_prod_bounds} follows analogously from summing $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\_}(X,Y,Z)$ over $\ye$, $\ya$ and $\yb$.
    The remaining claimed inequalities in \cref{eq:psum_yc_ye_uniform} are the result of similar summation identities.
    For example, summing the quantity $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$ over $\yf$ and applying \cref{eq:double_projs} produces
    \begin{align}
        \label{eq:psum_yf_over_yf}
        \begin{split}
            \sum_{\yf} \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)
            &= \sum_{\yf} \Tr[P^{\ye}_{\ya\yf} (\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi^{\yb\yd}_{\yf}(Y,Z))], \\
            &= \sum_{\yf} \Tr[P^{\ye}_{\ya\yb\yd}(\id_{\ya}\ot P^{\yf}_{\yb\yd}) (\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi_{\yb}(Y) \ot \Phi_{\yd}(Z))], \\
            &= \Tr[P^{\ye}_{\ya\yb\yd} (\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi_{\yb}(Y) \ot \Phi_{\yd}(Z))], \\
            &= \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\ye}(X,Y,Z).
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
    Therefore, from the non-negativity of $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$, we obtain $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z) \leq \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\ye}(X,Y,Z)$ which establishes one half of the lower bound on $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\ye}(X,Y,Z)$ in \cref{eq:psum_yc_ye_uniform}; the other half of the lower bound follows analogously from summing $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\_}(X,Y,Z)$ over $\yc$.
    Finally, for the upper bounds on $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\ye}(X,Y,Z)$ in \cref{eq:psum_yc_ye_uniform}, we remark that \cref{cor:bisummation} can be extended to handle more than two matrices by repeated application of \cref{cor:bisummation};
    specifically, by starting with \cref{eq:psum_yf_over_ye} and further summing over $\yf$ and applying \cref{eq:psum_yf_over_yf} and \cref{cor:bisummation}, we obtain
    \begin{align}
        \begin{split}
            \sum_{\ye} \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\ye}(X,Y,Z) 
            &= \sum_{\ye\yf} \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z), \\
            &= \sum_{\yf} \vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yf}^{\yb\yd}(Y,Z), \\
            &= \vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yb}(Y)\vp_{\yd}(Z),
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
    which is three matrix analogue of one half of \cref{cor:bisummation} and establishes the bound $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\ye}(X,Y,Z) \leq \vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yb}(Y)\vp_{\yd}(Z)$ because $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\ye}(X,Y,Z) \geq 0$.
    For the other half of the three matrix analogue of \cref{cor:bisummation}, we can again use \cref{eq:psum_yf_over_yf} and apply \cref{cor:bisummation} twice to obtain
    \begin{align}
        \begin{split}
            \sum_{\ya\yb\yd} \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\ye}(X,Y,Z)
            &= \sum_{\ya\yb\yd\yf} \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z), \\
            &= \sum_{\ya\yb\yd\yf} \Tr[P^{\ye}_{\ya\yf} (\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi^{\yb\yd}_{\yf}(Y,Z))], \\
            &= \sum_{\ya\yf} \Tr[P^{\ye}_{\ya\yf} (\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \sum_{\yb\yd}\Phi^{\yb\yd}_{\yf}(Y,Z))], \\
            &= \sum_{\ya\yf} \Tr[P^{\ye}_{\ya\yf} (\Phi_{\ya}(X) \ot \Phi_{\yf}(Y+Z))], \\
            &= \sum_{\ya\yf} \vp^{\ya\yf}_{\ye}(X, Y+Z), \\
            &= \vp_{\ye}(X+Y+Z),
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
    which in turn establishes the remaining inequality $\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\ye}(X,Y,Z) \leq \vp_{\ye}(X+Y+Z)$.
\end{proof}

We can now immediately prove the ``only if'' portion of the claim of \cref{thm:iff_tet}.
\begin{cor}
    \label{cor:tet_eigenvalue_cubic}
    Let $X,Y,Z \geq 0$ be positive semidefinite $n\times n$ matrices.
    Then for all triples of Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yd)$,
    \begin{equation}
        \vp_{\ya}(X)^{\frac{2}{3}}\vp_{\yb}(Y)\vp_{\yd}(Z)^{\frac{2}{3}}
        \leq \sum_{\yc,\ye,\yf} \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{\infty} \vp_{\yf}(Y+Z)^{\frac{1}{3}}\vp_{\yc}(X+Y)^{\frac{1}{3}}\vp_{\ye}(X+Y+Z)^{\frac{1}{3}}.
    \end{equation}
\end{cor}
\begin{proof}
    The claim follows by summing the inequality in \cref{lem:varphi_tet_main_ineq} over triples $(\yc,\ye,\yf)$ and relaxing both the sides of the resulting inequality. 
    For the lower bound, we have $\vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yb}(Y)\vp_{\yd}(Z) = \sum_{\yc\ye\yf}\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)$ which implies
    \begin{equation}
        \vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yb}(Y)\vp_{\yd}(Z) \leq \sum_{\yc\ye\yf} \abs{\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)}.
    \end{equation}
    Then for the upper bound, \cref{prop:tet_psum_ineq} implies 
    \begin{align}
        \begin{split}
            &\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\_}(X,Y,Z) \leq\\
            &\qquad\min\{ \vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yf}(Y+Z)\vp_{\yc}(X+Y)\vp_{\yd}(Z), \vp_{\ye}(X+Y+Z)^{2}, \vp_{\ya}(X)^2\vp_{\yb}(Y)^2\vp_{\yd}(Z)^2\}.
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
    Here we elect to remove the minimization by relaxing this inequality to the following:
    \begin{equation}
        \vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\_\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\_}(X,Y,Z) \leq \left[\vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yf}(Y+Z)\vp_{\yc}(X+Y)\vp_{\yd}(Z)\vp_{\ye}(X+Y+Z)\right]^{\frac{2}{3}}.
    \end{equation}
    Then the claimed result is obtained by applying \cref{lem:varphi_tet_main_ineq} and then dividing by a common factor of $(\vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yd}(Z))^{\frac{1}{3}}$ (note that whenever $\vp_{\ya}(X)\vp_{\yd}(Z) = 0$, the resuling inequality holds trivially).
\end{proof}
The proof of the ``if'' portion of \cref{thm:iff_tet} can be found in \cref{sec:dist_bound_for_tet_horn} which proves the distance bound of \cref{thm:approx_tet} (this implies the ``if'' portion of \cref{thm:iff_tet} after sending $k \to \infty$).

\subsection{The large \texorpdfstring{$\boldsymbol{n}$}{n} limit \& an entropic inequality}
\label{sec:large_n}

The purpose of this section is to show how to leverage the inverse polynomial bound on $\norm{\abcdefsymbol}$ for tetrahedral $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ provided by \cref{cor:asym_6j} to derive inequalities on the entropies of the eigenvalues.
Our strategy will be to prove an upper bound on the $6j$ symbol which depends only on the dimensions of the underlying irreducible representations and the multiplicity spaces between them.
By combining this upper bound with the lower bound provided by \cref{cor:asym_6j} (specifically the $n$-independent bound in \cref{eq:n_independent_inverse_poly}), we will prove the following.
\begin{thm}
    \label{thm:entropic_ineq}
    Let $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ be tetrahedral eigenvalues.
    Then
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:entropic_ineq}
        s(\ec) + s(\ef) - s(\eb) - s(\ee) \geq 0,
    \end{equation}
    where
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:little_s_defn}
        s(\ex) \coloneqq - \sum_{i=1}^{n} \ex_i \log \ex_i.
    \end{equation}
\end{thm}

We begin with our upper bound on the $6j$ symbol.
\begin{lem}
    \label{lem:sixj_ssa_bound}
    Let $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ be a sextuple of Young diagrams.
    Then
    \begin{equation}
        \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{2}^{2}
        \leq \left(c_{\ya,\yf}^{\ye}c_{\yb,\yd}^{\yf}c_{\ya,\yb}^{\yc}c_{\yc,\yd}^{\ye}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \frac{\dim(V_{\yc}^n) \dim(V_{\yf}^n)}{\dim(V_{\yb}^n) \dim(V_{\ye}^n)}.
    \end{equation}
\end{lem}
\begin{proof}
    Here we provide a diagrammatic proof of the claimed inequality.
    For the sake of clarity, we abbreviate the irreducible representation labels, $V_{\yx}^{n}$, and omit the multiplicity space labels on the intermediate steps.
    \begin{equation}
        \tikzfig{tikz/final/sixj_upper}
    \end{equation}
    The first equality follows from \cref{defn:sixj}.
    The inequality follows from an application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality with respect to the annotated contraction.
    The final equality follows from applying Schur's lemma to components of the diagram mapping $V_{\yb}^{n}$ to itself on the left and $V_{\ye}^{n}$ to itself on the right.
    Note that the factors of $\dim(V_{\ye}^{n})^2$ appearing on left-most and right-most sides of the above inequality cancel producing the claimed inequality.
\end{proof}

An immediate application of \cref{lem:sixj_ssa_bound} is to combine it with \cref{eq:n_independent_inverse_poly}.
If $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ are tetrahedral eigenvalues, then by \cref{cor:asym_6j} there exists a converging sequence of Young diagrams $(\ya_k, \yb_k, \yc_k, \yd_k, \ye_k, \yf_k)$ such that
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:combined_bound}
    \frac{1}{q(k)} \leq \norm{\tightcurlysixj{ \ya_k & \yb_k & \yc_k \\ \yd_k & \ye_k & \yf_k}}_{2}^2 \leq \left(c_{\ya_k,\yf_k}^{\ye_k}c_{\yb_k,\yd_k}^{\yf_k}c_{\ya_k,\yb_k}^{\yc_k}c_{\yc_k,\yd_k}^{\ye_k}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \frac{\dim(V_{\yc_k}^n) \dim(V_{\yf_k}^n)}{\dim(V_{\yb_k}^n) \dim(V_{\ye_k}^n)}.
\end{equation}
where $q(k)$ is some polynomial in $k$ independent of the dimension $n$ (see \cref{eq:n_independent_inverse_poly}).
Remarkably, the Littlewood-Richardson coefficients $c_{\ya_k,\yf_k}^{\ye_k}, c_{\yb_k,\yd_k}^{\yf_k}, c_{\ya_k,\yb_k}^{\yc_k}$, and $c_{\yc_k,\yd_k}^{\ye_k}$ are also \textit{independent} of the dimension parameter $n$ defining the group $\U(n)$ (assuming $n$ is at least as large as the maximum depth of any of the involved Young diagrams.
This observation provides us with an opportunity to optimize the above bound by varying the value of $n$.

In particular, it will useful to better understand how the dimension, $\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})$, of the irreducible representation $V_{\yx}^{n}$ of $\U(n)$ grows as $n$ becomes much larger than the number of rows of $\yx$.
In general, for fixed $\yx$, the function $n \mapsto V_{\yx}^{n}$ can grow at an exponential rate with increasing $n$.
One can find estimates for $\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})$ in the regime where $n$ is large compared to ${\syx}$ and also the last row $\yx_{\min}$ is large compared to the depth of $\yx$ in paper by \Citeauthor{itzykson1966unitary} \cite[Section III]{itzykson1966unitary}.
Here we use an elegant presentation of this scaling, which relates the asymptotics of $\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})$ to the value of $\dim(W_{\yx})$ (see \cite[Lemma 2]{harrow2023approximate} or \cite[Equations (25,26)]{childs2007weak}).
\begin{prop}
    \label{prop:harrow_formula}
    Let $\yx$ be a Young diagram with depth at most $n$. Then
    \begin{equation}
        \frac{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})}{n^{{\syx}}} = \frac{\dim(W_{\yx})}{{\syx}!}\prod_{(i,j) \in \yx} \left(1 + \frac{j-i}{n}\right).
    \end{equation}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    The statement follows from comparing the the Weyl dimension formula for $\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})$ given in \cref{eq:frob_dim} with the Frobenius dimension formula for $\dim(W_{\yx})$ given in \cite[Corollaries 9.1.5]{goodman2000representations}:
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:frob_dim}
        \dim(W_{\yx}) = \frac{{\syx}!\Delta(\yx + \rho)}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}(\yx+\rho)_i!}.
    \end{equation}
    That this formula for $\dim(W_{\yx})$ does not depend on $n$ for $n \geq \ell(\yx)$ and coincides with the Hook-length formula given earlier is proved by \cite[Corollary 9.1.6]{goodman2000representations}.
    For additional details, see also \cite[Appendix A]{harrow2023approximate}.
\end{proof}
From \cref{prop:harrow_formula} we obtain the following corollary.
\begin{cor}
    \label{cor:harrow_ssa_lim}
    Let $\yc,\yf,\yb,\ye$ be a quadruple of Young diagrams such that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:balanced_sizes}
        {\syc}+{\syf} = {\syb}+{\sye}.
    \end{equation}
    Then
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}
        \frac{\dim(V_{\yc}^n) \dim(V_{\yf}^n)}{\dim(V_{\yb}^n) \dim(V_{\ye}^n)} =
        \frac{{\syb}!{\sye}!}{{\syc}!{\syf}!}
        \frac{\dim(W_{\yc}) \dim(W_{\yf})}{\dim(W_{\yb}) \dim(W_{\ye})}
        = \frac{H_{\yb}H_{\ye}}{H_{\yc}H_{\yf}}
    \end{equation}
\end{cor}
\begin{proof}
    From \cref{prop:harrow_formula} one evidently has for any Young diagram $\yx$ the limit
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty} \frac{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})}{n^{{\syx}}} = \frac{\dim(W_{\yx})}{{\syx}!} = \frac{1}{H_{\yx}}
    \end{equation}
    where $H_{\yx}$ is the product of the hook lengths in $\yx$ as defined by \cref{eq:dim_W_hook_product}.
    The claim then follows because the missing prefactor, $\frac{n^{{\syb}}n^{{\sye}}}{n^{{\syc}}n^{{\syf}}}$, is equal to one by \cref{eq:balanced_sizes}.
\end{proof}
We are now in a position to prove the main result of this section.
\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:entropic_ineq}]
    As $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ are tetrahedral, the inverse polynomial bound on $\norm{\abcdefsymbol}$ for tetrahedral $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ provided by \cref{cor:asym_6j} implies it must at least be non-zero and thus \cref{eq:balanced_sizes} holds.
    Therefore, by \cref{cor:harrow_ssa_lim} and the discussion surrounding the bound in \cref{eq:combined_bound} we conclude that along the convergent sequence of Young diagrams,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:hook_ratio_limit}
        \lim_{k\to\infty} \frac{1}{k} \log \left(\frac{H_{\yb_k}H_{\ye_k}}{H_{\yc_k}H_{\yf_k}}\right) \geq 0
    \end{equation}
    provided the limit.
    To prove the limit exists, an application of Stirling's approximation, $\log n! \approx n \log n - n$ \cite{robbins1955remark}, to the approximation formula $H_{\yx} \approx \prod_{i=1}^{n}\yx_i!$ produces for any constant $\eta$ the formula
    \begin{equation}
        \frac{1}{\eta}\log H_{\yx} \approx (\log \eta - 1) \syx + \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\yx_i}{\eta}\log \frac{\yx_i}{\eta} 
    \end{equation}
    Because ${\syc}+{\syf} = {\syb}+{\sye}$ (\cref{eq:balanced_sizes}), the $(\log \eta - 1)$ terms will cancel in \cref{eq:hook_ratio_limit}.
    Then using the fact that that $(\yb_k, \yc_k, \ye_k, \yf_k)$ converge in proportion to the eigenvalues $(\eb,\ec,\ee,\ef)$ (with proportionality constant $\eta = \frac{k}{\tee}$), we conclude that \cref{eq:hook_ratio_limit} has a limit and moreover it equals the left-hand side of \cref{eq:entropic_ineq}. 
\end{proof}
\clearpage
\color{red!50!black}TC: THE FOLLOWING SECTION IS CURRENTLY BEING RE-REWRITTEN INTO PREVIOUS SECTIONS
\section{Distance bounds via eigenvalue concentration}
\label{sec:distance_bounds}

The purpose of this section is to provide a proof of the distance bound in \cref{thm:approx_tet}.
With \cref{thm:approx_tet} established, the ``if'' portion of \cref{thm:iff_tet} follows immediately.
Note that ``only if'' portion of \cref{thm:iff_tet} is just a restatement of \cref{cor:tet_eigenvalue_cubic} proven in the previous sections.

These bounds make use of eigenvalue concentration techniques, which we review first in \cref{sec:eig_est_and_sep}, as well as formulas from random matrix theory, which we review afterwards in \cref{sec:random_matrices}.
As a warm-up exercise, in \cref{sec:dist_bound_for_orig_horn} we demonstrate how these techniques can be applied to obtain a distance bound for the original Horn problem.
Then we generalize the proof technique in \cref{sec:dist_bound_for_tet_horn} to prove \cref{thm:approx_tet}.

\subsection{Eigenvalue estimation \& separation}
\label{sec:eig_est_and_sep}

In this section we show how the representations $\pi_{\yx}(X)$ of Hermitian matrices $X$ encode useful information about the eigenvalues of $X$.
These results apply to the case where $X \geq 0$ is a positive semidefinite matrix, in which case the representation $\pi_{\yx}(X) \geq 0$ is positive semidefinite also.
Thus whenever $X \geq 0$, one can define a probability distribution known as the \textit{Schur-Weyl} distribution \cite{odonnell2016efficient}.
\begin{defn}
    The Schur-Weyl distribution of order $k$ for a positive semidefinite matrix $X \geq 0$ with eigenvalues $\ex = (\ex_1, \ldots, \ex_n)$ is defined as the probability distribution over Young diagrams $\yx$ with $k$ boxes with probability
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:schur_weyl}
        p_{\ex}(\yx) \coloneqq \frac{\Tr[P_{\yx} X^{\ot k}]}{\Tr[X^{\ot k}]} = \frac{\dim W_{\yx} s_{\yx}(\ex)}{\tex^{k}} = \frac{\syx! }{\tex^{k}}\vp_{\yx}(\ex).
    \end{equation}
\end{defn}
A key property of the Schur-Weyl distribution $p_{\ex}(\yx)$ is that it behaves similarly to the multinomial distribution $m_{\ex}(\yx)$ where
\begin{equation}
    m_{\ex}(\yx) \coloneqq \frac{\syx!}{\tex^{k}}\frac{\ex_1^{\yx_1}}{\yx_1!}\frac{\ex_2^{\yx_2}}{\yx_2!}\cdots \frac{\ex_n^{\yx_n}}{\yx_n!}.
\end{equation}
\begin{prop}
    \label{prop:multinomial_bound}
    Given a Young diagram $\yx$ and non-negative eigenvalues $\ex$,
    \begin{equation}
        p_{\ex}(\yx) \leq \dim V_{\yx}^{n} m_{\ex}(\yx).
    \end{equation}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    The proof follows from (i) an upper bound on the Schur-polynomial $s_{\yx}(\ex)$,
    \begin{equation}
        s_{\yx}(\ex) \leq \dim V_{\yx}^{n} \ex_1^{\yx_1}\ex_2^{\yx_2}\cdots \ex_n^{\yx_n},
    \end{equation}
    which follows because $s_{\yx}(\ex)$ can be expressed as a sum over $\dim V_{\yx}^{n}$ multinomials of the form $\ex_1^{\mu_1}\ex_2^{\mu_2}\cdots \ex_n^{\mu_n}$ with $\ex_1^{\yx_1}\ex_2^{\yx_2}\cdots \ex_n^{\yx_n}$ the largest, and (ii) a lower bound on the Hook-product,
    \begin{equation}
        H_{\yx} \geq \yx_1!\cdots \yx_n!,
    \end{equation}
    which follows because $h_{\yx}(b) \geq \yx_i - j + 1$ holds for the box $b$ in row $i$ and column $j$.
\end{proof}
\begin{rem}
    We will henceforth relax the dimension prefactor $\dim V_{\yx}^{n}$ using the following dimension bound which holds for all Young diagrams with $k$ boxes:
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:unitary_irrep_dim_bound}
        \dim V_{\yx}^{n} \leq \polyk{\binom{n}{2}} \coloneqq (k+1)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}.
    \end{equation}
\end{rem}
The following is known result is known as eigenvalue or spectrum estimation \cite{alicki1988symmetry,keyl2001estimating,hayashi2002quantum,christandl2006spectra,odonnell2016efficient}.
\begin{prop}[Eigenvalue estimation]
    \label{prop:eig_est}
    Let $X \geq 0$ be a positive semidefinite $n\times n$ matrix with eigenvalues $\ex = (\ex_1 \geq \cdots \geq \ex_n \geq 0)$
    Then the Schur-Weyl distribution $p_{\ex}(\yx)$ for $\yx \vdash k$ satisfies
    \begin{equation}
        p_{\ex}(\yx) \leq \polyk{\binom{n}{2}} \exp\left(-k \kl{\frac{\yx}{k}}{\frac{\ex}{\tex}}\right)
    \end{equation}
    where $\kl{p}{q}$ denotes the Kullback-Leibler divergence of a distribution $q$ from a distribution $p$:
    \begin{equation}
        \kl{p}{q} = \sum_{i=1}^{n} p_i \log \frac{p_i}{q_i}.
    \end{equation}
\end{prop}
The Kullback-Leibler divergence, $\kl{p}{q}$, is a non-negative quantity which vanishes if and only if $p=q$.
Consequently, eigenvalue estimation captures the fact that, like the multinomial distribution $m_x(\yx)$, the Schur-Weyl distribution $p_{\ex}(\yx)$ is sharply concentrated around Young diagrams $\yx$ whose distribution, $\yx/k$, is similar to the eigenvalue distribution $\ex/\tex$.

For our purposes, it will instead be useful to derive a result which compares the eigenvalues of a pair of positive semidefinite matrices $X,Y \geq 0$ directly.
Recall that the \textit{fidelity} $F(p,q) \in [0,1]$ between two probability distributions $p$ and $q$ over a common set $\{1,\ldots,r\}$ is defined by $F(p,q) = \sum_{i=1}^{r}\sqrt{p_iq_i}$ and is equal one if and only if $p = q$.
For notational convenient, we will extend this definition of fidelity to consider unnormalized distributions in the following way.
Suppose $p = (p_1, \ldots, p_r)$ and $q = (q_1, \ldots, q_r)$ are non-negative tuples over the set $\{1,\ldots,r\}$ which are not necessarily normalized, but nevertheless have the same total $\tau = \sum_{i=1}^{r} p_i = \sum_{i=1}^{r} q_i$.
Then we define the \textit{unnormalized fidelity} $F(p,q) \in [0,\tau]$ between $p$ and $q$ by
\begin{equation}
    F(p,q) = \sum_{i=1}^{r}\sqrt{p_iq_i},
\end{equation}
where $F(p,q) = \tau$ if and only if $p=q$.
The unnormalized fidelity inherits all of its properties from the normalized fidelity by homogeneity:
\begin{equation}
    F(p,q) = \tau F\left( \frac{p}{\tau}, \frac{q}{\tau} \right).
\end{equation}

\begin{lem}[Eigenvalue separation]
    \label{lem:eig_sep}
    Let $\ex$ and $\ey$ non-negative eigenvalues of length $n$ with the same trace $\tau = \tex = \tey$.
    For any non-negative integer $k\geq 1$,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:eig_sep}
        \sum_{\yx \vdash k}\sqrt{\vp_{\yx}(\ex)\vp_{\yx}(\ey)} \leq \polyk{\binom{n}{2}} \frac{F(\ex,\ey)^{k}}{k!},
    \end{equation}
    where $F(\ex,\ey)$ is fidelity between the eigenvalues $\ex$ and $\ey$ and $F(\ex, \ey) = \tau$ if and only if $\ex = \ey$.
\end{lem}
\begin{proof}
    We may assume that $\ex, \ey$ are non-zero and thus $\tau > 0$.
    The left-hand side of \cref{eq:eig_sep} is the equal to the unnormalized fidelity between $\vp_{\yx}(\ex)$ and $\vp_{\yx}(\ey)$.
    As $\vp_{\yx}(\ex)$ is proportional to the Schur-Weyl distribution $p_{\ex}(\yx)$ by definition (\cref{eq:schur_weyl}), we have
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\yx \vdash k}\sqrt{\vp_{\yx}(\ex)\vp_{\yx}(\ey)} = \frac{\tau^k}{k!}\sum_{\yx \vdash k} \sqrt{p_{\ex}(\yx)p_{\ex}(\yx)} = \frac{\tau^k}{k!}F_k(p_{\ex},p_{\ey})
    \end{equation}
    where $F_k(p_{\ex},p_{\ey})$ denotes the normalized fidelity between the Schur-Weyl distributions $p_{\ex}$ and $p_{\ey}$ over Young diagrams of size $k$.
    From \cref{prop:multinomial_bound} and \cref{eq:unitary_irrep_dim_bound}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        F_k(p_{\ex},p_{\ey}) \leq \polyk{\binom{n}{2}} F_k(m_x,m_y).
    \end{equation}
    where $F_k(m_\ex,m_\ey)$ is the fidelity between the multinomial distributions $m_{\ex}$ and $m_{\ey}$.
    Since fidelities are multiplicative with respect to multinomial distributions, i.e.,
    \begin{equation}
        F_k(m_x, m_y) = F\left(\frac{\ex}{\tau}, \frac{\ey}{\tau}\right)^{k} = \frac{F(\ex, \ey)^k}{\tau^k},
    \end{equation}
    we conclude
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\yx \vdash k}\sqrt{\vp_{\yx}(\ex)\vp_{\yx}(\ey)} \leq \frac{\tau^k}{k!}\polyk{\binom{n}{2}}\frac{F(\ex, \ey)^k}{\tau^k}
    \end{equation}
    which is equal to \cref{eq:eig_sep}.
\end{proof}
In subsequent sections we will frequently use of the \textit{weaker} bound
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:weak_eig_sep}
    \forall \yx \vdash k: \qquad \sqrt{\vp_{\yx}(x)\vp_{\yx}(y)} \leq \polyk{\binom{n}{2}} \frac{F(x,y)^{k}}{k!},
\end{equation}
which follows from \cref{lem:eig_sep} because $\vp_{\yx}(x), \vp_{\yx}(y) \geq 0$ whenever the eigenvalues $x,y$ are non-negative.
\begin{rem}[Fidelities \& Distances]
    In order to quantify the separation between distributions more geometrically, consider the Kolmogorov (or total variation) distance between two normalized probabibility distributions $p$ and $q$:
    \begin{equation}
        K(p,q) \coloneqq \frac{1}{2}\norm{p-q}_1.
    \end{equation}
    The normalized fidelity $F(p,q)$ and Kolmogorov distance $K(p,q)$ are related by the inequalities
    \begin{equation}
        1-F(p,q) \leq K(p,q) \leq \sqrt{1-F(p,q)^2}
    \end{equation}
    For a proof, see for example \cite[Proposition 5, Equation (41)]{fuchs2002cryptographic}.
    For the purposes of this paper, we only need the bound $K(p,q) \leq \sqrt{1-F(p,q)^2}$ which is equivalent to $F(p,q) \leq \sqrt{1-K(p,q)^2}$ which can be relaxed to obtain\footnote{Here we used the inequality $\sqrt{1-x^2} \leq \exp(-x^2/2)$ which holds for all $x \in [0,1]$.}
    \begin{equation}
        F(p,q) \leq \exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}K(p,q)^2\right) = \exp\left(-\frac{1}{8}\norm{p-q}_1^2\right).
    \end{equation}
    Stated in terms of the unnormalized fidelity $F(p,q)$ between unnormalized distributions $p,q$ with the same total $\tau$, this inequality is equivalent to
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:fidelity_vs_distance}
        \frac{F(p,q)}{\tau} \leq \exp\left(-\frac{1}{8\tau^2}\norm{p-q}_1^2\right).
    \end{equation}
    In subsequent sections we will use \cref{eq:fidelity_vs_distance} to convert lower bounds on fidelities $F(p,q)$ into upper bounds on distances $\norm{p-q}_1$.
\end{rem}

\subsection{Results for random matrices}
\label{sec:random_matrices}

The purpose of this section is record some results concerning sums of random Hermitian matrices with fixed eigenvalues sampled uniformly from their respective orbits.
The first two propositions follow from standard applications of Schur's lemma, and are used to prove the others.
\begin{prop}
    Let $\ex = (\ex_1 \geq \cdots \geq \ex_n)$ be a set of eigenvalues and let $X \sim \orbit_{\ex}$ denote a random Hermitian matrix of the form $X = U[\ex]U^{-1}$ where $U \in \U(n)$ is sampled uniformly with respect to the normalized Haar measure on $\U(n)$.
    Then for any Young diagram $\yx$,
    \begin{equation}
        \expect_{X \sim \orbit_{\ex}} \pi_{\yx}(X) = s_{\yx}(\ex)\frac{\id_{V_{\yx}^{n}}}{\dim V_{\yx}^{n}}.
    \end{equation}
\end{prop}
\begin{cor}
    \label{cor:random_sum}
    Let $(\ex,\ey)$ be a pair of eigenvalues. Then
    \begin{equation}
        \expect_{\substack{(X,Y) \sim \\ \orbit_{\ex} \times \orbit_{\ey}}} 
        \frac{\vp^{\ya\yb}_{\yc}(X,Y)}{\dim V_{\yc}^{n}} = c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \frac{\vp_{\ya}(\ex)}{\dim V_{\ya}^{n}}\frac{\vp_{\yb}(\ey)}{\dim V_{\yb}^{n}} .
    \end{equation}
\end{cor}
\begin{cor}
    \label{cor:random_matrices_tet}
    Let $(x,y,z)$ be a triple of eigenvalues. Then
    \begin{equation} 
        \expect_{\substack{(X,Y,Z) \sim \\ \orbit_{\ex}\times \orbit_{\ey} \times \orbit_{\ez}}} \frac{\vp^{\ya\yb\yd}_{\yc\ye\yf}(X,Y,Z)}{\dim V_{\ye}^{n}} 
        = \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{2}^{2} \frac{\vp_{\ya}(x)}{\dim V_{\ya}^{n}}\frac{\vp_{\yb}(y)}{\dim V_{\yb}^{n}}\frac{\vp_{\yd}(z)}{\dim V_{\yd}^{n}}.
    \end{equation}
\end{cor}
% Moreover in our proof of \cref{cor:asym_6j}, we will use another measure, known as the Hilbert-Schmidt measure, over the compact space of trace $\tau$ positive semidefinite Hermitian matrices \cite{zyczkowski2001induced}.
% This measure is a product measure with respect to eigenvalues and eigenvectors and uses the $\U(n)$ Haar measure for the eigenvectors and the unique probability measure for the eigenvalues $x$ with density proportional to the squared Vandermonde determinant $\Delta(x)^2$ \cite{zyczkowski2001induced}.
% It can equivalently be characterized in terms of the pushforward of applying the map $\psi \mapsto \tau \Tr_{n}(\psi\psi^{\dagger})$ to $\U(n^2)$-Haar random unit vectors $\psi$ in $\mathbb C^{n} \ot \mathbb C^{n}$ \cite{zyczkowski2001induced}.
